I mostly just feel uncomfortable and try to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what and how much you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Once I start, there’s usually a point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else anyway. It feels like the decision has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the late-night eating.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you normally would, and feeling like you can't stop, ever happen during the day when you're not working late?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mostly notice it when I’m up late, so I can’t say I’ve seen the same thing happening during the day.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It feels like at some point I’m watching myself do it, but the choice to stop isn’t really in my hands anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I don’t use laxatives after. I mostly just feel uncomfortable and try to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what and how much you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Once I start, there’s usually a point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else anyway. It feels like the decision has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the late-night eating.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you normally would, and feeling like you can't stop, ever happen during the day when you're not working late?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mostly notice it when I’m up late, so I can’t say I’ve seen the same thing happening during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat more during these late nights because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really connected it to a specific feeling like stress or anxiety. It’s more just that I’m awake late and then I start eating, but I don’t always know what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time during the day, not just at night?\n\nPatient: Not really during the day, at least not that I’ve noticed. The main thing that brings me in is the late work nights—I'll order what I call dinner, but then it turns into an entrée, sides, dessert, and then more snacks from my kitchen. It’s way more than I’d eat if someone were sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by feeling bored, stressed, or emotional in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern like, “I’m stressed, so I eat.” It might be connected to being tired or just being up late with nothing else going on, but I can’t really say it’s boredom or emotions for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally upset or anxious right before these late-night eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not right before. I mean, I’m usually tired, and maybe kind of wound up from being awake late, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially upset or anxious before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes might be related to feeling lonely since you mentioned you'd eat less if someone were with you?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think the bigger thing is embarrassment. If I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m really careful to eat lightly and look normal. But when I’m alone at my condo after midnight, that’s when it happens. I’ll close the blinds, and I usually throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be mortified if anyone saw how much food there was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes because you're physically hungry or because you feel an overwhelming urge to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really physical hunger, at least not usually. It’s more like once I start, there’s this point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else. It feels like the choice has kind of slipped away from me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

{"intent": "determine if the patient's eating episodes are related to a specific emotional state", "question": "Do you think your late-night eating episodes are ever triggered by feelings of emptiness or a lack of fulfillment?"}

I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it ends up being a full entrée, sides, dessert, and then I keep grabbing extra snacks from my kitchen. It’s way more than I’d normally eat if someone were there with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I know I don’t feel good about it afterward, but I haven’t really sorted out whether it’s guilt specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll be sitting at my desk with my Illustrator files open, and I just kind of move from one container to the next without really tasting it. Then I look down and realize I’ve eaten most of it in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’ll think, “I should stop now,” but then I’m still reaching for more or opening another package. It feels like at some point I’m watching myself do it, but the choice to stop isn’t really in my hands anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I don’t use laxatives after. I mostly just feel uncomfortable and try to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what and how much you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Once I start, there’s usually a point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else anyway. It feels like the decision has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the late-night eating.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you normally would, and feeling like you can't stop, ever happen during the day when you're not working late?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mostly notice it when I’m up late, so I can’t say I’ve seen the same thing happening during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat more during these late nights because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really connected it to a specific feeling like stress or anxiety. [
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because my eating has gotten kind of out of hand, especially on late work nights. I’ll order what I tell myself is just dinner, but then it ends up being a full entrée, sides, dessert, and then I keep grabbing extra snacks from my kitchen. It’s way more than I’d normally eat if someone were there with me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I know I don’t feel good about it afterward, but I haven’t really sorted out whether it’s guilt specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I’ll be sitting at my desk with my Illustrator files open, and I just kind of move from one container to the next without really tasting it. Then I look down and realize I’ve eaten most of it in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’ll think, “I should stop now,” but then I’m still reaching for more or opening another package. It feels like at some point I’m watching myself do it, but the choice to stop isn’t really in my hands anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I don’t use laxatives after. I mostly just feel uncomfortable and try to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what and how much you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Once I start, there’s usually a point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else anyway. It feels like the decision has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the late-night eating.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you normally would, and feeling like you can't stop, ever happen during the day when you're not working late?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mostly notice it when I’m up late, so I can’t say I’ve seen the same thing happening during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat more during these late nights because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really connected it to a specific feeling like stress or anxiety. It’s more just that I’m awake late and then I start eating, but I don’t always know what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time during the day, not just at night?\n\nPatient: Not really during the day, at least not that I’ve noticed. The main thing that brings me in is the late work nights—I'll order what I call dinner, but then it turns into an entrée, sides, dessert, and then more snacks from my kitchen. It’s way more than I’d eat if someone were sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by feeling bored, stressed, or emotional in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern like, “I’m stressed, so I eat.” It might be connected to being tired or just being up late with nothing else going on, but I can’t really say it’s boredom or emotions for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally upset or anxious right before these late-night eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not right before. I mean, I’m usually tired, and maybe kind of wound up from being awake late, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially upset or anxious before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes might be related to feeling lonely since you mentioned you'd eat less if someone were with you?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think the bigger thing is embarrassment. If I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m really careful to eat lightly and look normal. But when I’m alone at my condo after midnight, that’s when it happens. I’ll close the blinds, and I usually throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be mortified if anyone saw how much food there was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes because you're physically hungry or because you feel an overwhelming urge to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really physical hunger, at least not usually. It’s more like once I start, there’s this point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else. It feels like the choice has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are ever triggered by feelings of emptiness or a lack of fulfillment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it as emptiness or lack of fulfillment. It feels more like I’m alone and it’s late, and then I start eating. I can’t say I’ve noticed a specific feeling like that right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this way if you were working late at night with someone else present?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. If someone else were there, like a coworker, I’d be very careful and probably just eat something small or normal. It’s when I’m alone at my condo late at night that I let it happen, and I’m honestly embarrassed by how much it is. I’ll even close the blinds and get rid of the containers fast so no one sees.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating is ever a way to cope with difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it coping with difficult emotions exactly. I haven’t really connected it to being upset or sad beforehand. It’s more that I’m alone, it’s late, and then the eating just kind of starts and gets hard to stop.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It feels like the decision has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the late-night eating.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you normally would, and feeling like you can't stop, ever happen during the day when you're not working late?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mostly notice it when I’m up late, so I can’t say I’ve seen the same thing happening during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat more during these late nights because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really connected it to a specific feeling like stress or anxiety. It’s more just that I’m awake late and then I start eating, but I don’t always know what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time during the day, not just at night?\n\nPatient: Not really during the day, at least not that I’ve noticed. The main thing that brings me in is the late work nights—I'll order what I call dinner, but then it turns into an entrée, sides, dessert, and then more snacks from my kitchen. It’s way more than I’d eat if someone were sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by feeling bored, stressed, or emotional in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern like, “I’m stressed, so I eat.” It might be connected to being tired or just being up late with nothing else going on, but I can’t really say it’s boredom or emotions for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally upset or anxious right before these late-night eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not right before. I mean, I’m usually tired, and maybe kind of wound up from being awake late, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially upset or anxious before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes might be related to feeling lonely since you mentioned you'd eat less if someone were with you?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think the bigger thing is embarrassment. If I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m really careful to eat lightly and look normal. But when I’m alone at my condo after midnight, that’s when it happens. I’ll close the blinds, and I usually throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be mortified if anyone saw how much food there was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes because you're physically hungry or because you feel an overwhelming urge to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really physical hunger, at least not usually. It’s more like once I start, there’s this point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else. It feels like the choice has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are ever triggered by feelings of emptiness or a lack of fulfillment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it as emptiness or lack of fulfillment. It feels more like I’m alone and it’s late, and then I start eating. I can’t say I’ve noticed a specific feeling like that right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this way if you were working late at night with someone else present?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. If someone else were there, like a coworker, I’d be very careful and probably just eat something small or normal. It’s when I’m alone at my condo late at night that I let it happen, and I’m honestly embarrassed by how much it is. I’ll even close the blinds and get rid of the containers fast so no one sees.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating is ever a way to cope with difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it coping with difficult emotions exactly. I haven’t really connected it to being upset or sad beforehand. It’s more that I’m alone, it’s late, and then the eating just kind of starts and gets hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these late-night eating episodes regularly, like on most late work nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s pretty regular. When I look back at my delivery receipts and my calendar, it’s usually at least once a week that it happens, sometimes more. And that’s been going on for several months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I don’t use laxatives after. Once I start, there’s usually a point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else anyway. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the late-night eating.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you normally would, and feeling like you can't stop, ever happen during the day when you're not working late?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mostly notice it when I’m up late, so I can’t say I’ve seen the same thing happening during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat more during these late nights because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really connected it to a specific feeling like stress or anxiety. If someone else were there, like a coworker, I’d be very careful and probably just eat something small or normal. It’s when I’m alone at my condo late at night that I let it happen, and I’m honestly embarrassed by how much it is. I’ll even close the blinds and get rid of the containers fast so no one sees.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating is ever a way to cope with difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it coping with difficult emotions exactly. I haven’t really connected it to being upset or sad beforehand. It’s more that I’m alone, it’s late, and then the eating just kind of starts and gets hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these late-night eating episodes regularly, like on most late work nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s pretty regular. When I look back at my delivery receipts and my calendar, it’s usually at least once a week that it happens, sometimes more. And that’s been going on for several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you had a more structured routine on late work nights?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Then I look down and realize I’ve eaten most of it in just a few minutes.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can stop eating once you've started on those late nights?\n\nPatient: Not really. I’ll think, “I should stop now,” but then I’m still reaching for more or opening another package. It feels like at some point I’m watching myself do it, but the choice to stop isn’t really in my hands anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit or use laxatives after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t make myself throw up, and I don’t use laxatives after. I mostly just feel uncomfortable and try to move on.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're in control of what and how much you're eating during those late-night episodes?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Once I start, there’s usually a point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else anyway. It feels like the decision has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over time due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t been tracking it closely, so I can’t say for sure that there’s been a significant change because of the late-night eating.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating more than you normally would, and feeling like you can't stop, ever happen during the day when you're not working late?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mostly notice it when I’m up late, so I can’t say I’ve seen the same thing happening during the day.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat more during these late nights because you're feeling stressed, anxious, or upset about something?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really connected it to a specific feeling like stress or anxiety. It’s more just that I’m awake late and then I start eating, but I don’t always know what’s behind it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes of eating a large amount of food in a short time during the day, not just at night?\n\nPatient: Not really during the day, at least not that I’ve noticed. The main thing that brings me in is the late work nights—I'll order what I call dinner, but then it turns into an entrée, sides, dessert, and then more snacks from my kitchen. It’s way more than I’d eat if someone were sitting across from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are triggered by feeling bored, stressed, or emotional in any way?\n\nPatient: I’m honestly not sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern like, “I’m stressed, so I eat.” It might be connected to being tired or just being up late with nothing else going on, but I can’t really say it’s boredom or emotions for sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel emotionally upset or anxious right before these late-night eating episodes start?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not right before. I mean, I’m usually tired, and maybe kind of wound up from being awake late, but I haven’t noticed feeling especially upset or anxious before it starts.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes might be related to feeling lonely since you mentioned you'd eat less if someone were with you?\n\nPatient: Maybe, but I think the bigger thing is embarrassment. If I’m with coworkers or friends, I’m really careful to eat lightly and look normal. But when I’m alone at my condo after midnight, that’s when it happens. I’ll close the blinds, and I usually throw out the delivery containers pretty quickly because I’d be mortified if anyone saw how much food there was.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to control what you're eating during these episodes because you're physically hungry or because you feel an overwhelming urge to continue eating?\n\nPatient: It’s not really physical hunger, at least not usually. It’s more like once I start, there’s this point where I know I should stop, but I keep going back to the food or opening something else. It feels like the choice has kind of slipped away from me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating episodes are ever triggered by feelings of emptiness or a lack of fulfillment?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I haven’t really thought of it as emptiness or lack of fulfillment. It feels more like I’m alone and it’s late, and then I start eating. I can’t say I’ve noticed a specific feeling like that right before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would eat this way if you were working late at night with someone else present?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. If someone else were there, like a coworker, I’d be very careful and probably just eat something small or normal. It’s when I’m alone at my condo late at night that I let it happen, and I’m honestly embarrassed by how much it is. I’ll even close the blinds and get rid of the containers fast so no one sees.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your late-night eating is ever a way to cope with difficult emotions?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it coping with difficult emotions exactly. I haven’t really connected it to being upset or sad beforehand. It’s more that I’m alone, it’s late, and then the eating just kind of starts and gets hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you have these late-night eating episodes regularly, like on most late work nights?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think it’s pretty regular. When I look back at my delivery receipts and my calendar, it’s usually at least once a week that it happens, sometimes more. And that’s been going on for several months.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits would change if you had a more structured routine on late work nights?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I haven’t had a very consistent routine on those nights, so it’s hard to say. I think structure might help, but I don’t know for certain.\n\nDoctor: Do you think having a more regular daily schedule would help reduce the frequency of these late-night eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, maybe it would help, but I haven’t really had a regular enough schedule to know. On nights when things are less predictable, it does seem easier for it to happen, but I can’t say for sure that a set schedule would stop it."
